Aghora: Book II: Kundalini

THE SACRED SHRI YANTRA is the most well known and fascinating of all yantras because of its intriciate geometric harmony. It consists of nine interlocking triangles, the union of five downward pointing yoni triangles (Shakti) with four upward pointing fire triangles (Shiva), and is also known as the Navayoni (Nine Yoni) chakra. The configuration produces a pattern of forty-three triangles which represent the goddess Tripurasundari at the center surrounded by her retinue of forty-two deities. These triangles are arranged in a series of five chakras that house one, eight, ten, ten and fourteen triangles progressively from the center outwards. This structure is enclosed by two concentric lotus circles of eight and sixteen petals enclosed within a square Bhupura or ground plan. The Mahavidya goddess Tripurasundari is also known as Shodashi, ‘the divine sixteen-year old goddess.’ She is of a beautiful red complexion with four arms holding a bow, noose, arrow and trident hook. She sits astride the prone body of her white Shiva in sexual union, supported by a throne whose four pedestals are the gods Brahma, Vishnu, Rudra and Indra. This form of the Shri Yantra was worshiped for many decades by Telang Swami in the Annapurna temple of Benares.

Aghora: Book II: Kundalini

**FOREWORD**

AGHORA, DESCRIBED IN THIS VOLUME as “super-tantra,” is a Path of Devotion to the Great Mother Goddess Kundalini, here manifesting with the Name and Image of the Goddess Tara. This Way is one of extraordinary extremes and intensities, even for tantra, and its aim is nothing less than to destroy the human limitations of the practitioner, so that he or she becomes a superhuman—in fact, a kind of deity.

*Aghora II* is the second volume of a trilogy describing and explicating this Way as practiced by the Aghori Vimalananda, and as recounted by his student, the ayurvedic physician Robert Svoboda. The result is certainly one of the most unusual works to be encountered in the whole wonder-filled literature of world religions. It is also a work very much of this time, shredding and trampling on universal and almost-universal prohibitions. The Aghori sets out to overcome human limitations by shattering internally every restraint, no matter how ancient or powerful the taboo, and also by creating a body/mind that is able to contain emotional, sensory and other experiences which would consume anyone not properly prepared.

The Aghori, as presented by Vimalananda, must literally and systematically create a body (and subtle bodies) with a nervous system and other systems able to explore levels of consciousness and to tolerate kinds and intensities of energies which would otherwise result in madness, terrible suffering, destruction of gross and subtle body/mind organs, and death. Crucial to avoiding those outcomes are access to the necessary esoteric teachings and trainings and, above all, to the Goddess.

As Vimalananda says again and again, in this present time the external forms of the spiritual disciplines are of much lesser importance than the inner work and, above all, the immediate and personal relationship to deity. It is possible to relate to the Great Mother Goddess in such a way that most of the requirements, temporal and material, of a spiritual discipline may be cast aside. However, what is dispensed with in the way of formalities must be compensated for by duration and intensity of both Practice and Devotion. Much present-day ritual and ceremony can be understood as aids or even crutches of a kind for those who lack the courage, dedication and devotion to proceed in unmediated interaction with Deity.

In speaking of intensities involved in such practice, here reference is to something going well beyond, for example, the “dionysiac element” in religions as experienced in practice. Those intensities are almost always reserved for special occasions, limited in time, and experienced within the context of different kinds of protections. But the Aghori, or similar figure, must always be willing to do without human supports of any kind and to venture into the awe-ful worlds of the nonhuman supported only by activated latent potentials and by confident belief that the Mother Goddess will guide and protect. Such a practitioner must be willing to venture into anything or nothing, knowing that in either case a successful experience will bring further isolation from the human and immersion in realities from which there can be no turning back. We are indeed speaking here of a “cosmic thrill-seeker” at home in realms of either terror or rapture—an ultimate psychenaut who is also our most authentic contemporary saint.

Despite the uniqueness and emphasis on inner work, there is in this book an enormous wealth of information of value to the student of Eastern spiritual disciplines. Often even a sentence or two will shed more significant light on profound states of consciousness or very complex stages of meditation than the reader is likely to find in whole volumes intended to illumine the same subjects. The book is of value to advanced practitioners of yoga and other paths of self-actualization while, at the same time, it should be fascinating reading for anyone interested in the farther reaches of human experiences and human potentials.

Let it be added that there is much in this volume, and more in its predecessor, that will be startling and shocking to almost all. How much is to be taken as “objective truth,” and how much is to be taken as “subjective truth,” often is unclear. Vimalananda makes it plain that this distinction is for him not one of great importance—and, if he is going to make a judgment in the matter, then “subjective truth” is doubtless of greater value. Vimalananda also belongs in the tradition of spiritual teachers who deliberately speak and behave in such a manner as to shock, and often dismay, the observer. Such Teachers are to be found in many Traditions—for our own time, the very saintly Fourth Way Master G. I. Gurdjieff is a wonderful example.

There are other Paths or Ways which have strong similarities to Aghora—most notably, ones associated with Kundalini and the Great Mother Goddess in some other religions and esoteric Traditions. I would particularly mention as examples the ancient and still-enduring esoteric Schools of the Goddess Sekhmet in Egypt, the Goddess Hera in Greece, the Goddess Kapo in Hawaii, the Goddess Kali in India—and there are also others. Each of these powerful and authentic mystery Traditions offers Paths of equal intensity, equally demanding of dedication and devotion to the Great Mother. Each uncompromisingly requires that the subtle and gross body/mind systems of the person become transmuted into vehicles or instruments having experiential capacities which by any ordinary standards are “superhuman.” Only when this has been accomplished can there be that level of interaction with deity and other nonhuman beings which the higher Work requires.

Finally, I feel obliged to say that I do not know whether Vimalananda was an actual person. His name, and other names in the book, are said to be pseudonyms intended to prevent readers from being “distracted” by “externals.” We are told that Vimalananda died about ten years ago, so that there is no question of searching him out, as many tried to do with another such mysterious character, the shaman Don Juan in the books of Carlos Castanada.

In personal conversation with the author, he states that Vimalananda was indeed an actual person and that the mystery surrounding his identity is mainly at the insistence of Vimalananda’s own family. Considering the shock content of various of the Teachings, that is quite understandable. Some may wish to pursue this matter further. Most will find it sufficient to benefit from the extraordinary contents of *Aghora II* and its predecessor.

As for myself, I take pleasure in writing this Foreword even in the absence of any verifiable certainties about the Aghori Vimalananda, and despite strong personal reservations concerning some of the practices described. What I do know from many years of first-hand experience is the immense power of the Way of Devotion to the Great Mother Goddess and how, as this book sets forth, such a Way differs from those which are more dependent on human teachings and such objectifications in the world as ceremonies and rituals. From my own experiential knowledge of the Fifth Way of the Goddess Sekhmet, I recognize in what is here called ‘’Aghora” another Path that has been provided as a real and powerful means of unveiling truth and effecting transformation.

**Robert Masters**, Ph.D. Co-Founder and Director of Research, The Foundation for Mind Research, Pomona, NY; author of *The Varieties of Psychedelic Experience*, *Forbidden Sexual Behavior and Morality*, *The Goddess Sekhmet* and many other books.

Aghora: Book II: Kundalini

**INTRODUCTION TO THE SECOND EDITION**

THE MORE THINGS CHANGE, THE MORE THEY REMAIN THE SAME. A quarter of a century has passed since I wrote in the Introduction to *Aghora II* that “information has become an article of commerce,” at a time when I had no idea of just how prominently commercial information was to become in today’s world. Copious material dealing with all sorts of mysteries is now available at a click or a touch—though now, as then, what is so easily possessed is not so easily comprehended. And accurate knowledge is in our era of “alternative facts” often buried under heaps of information-chaff generated by the unremittingly self-absorbed to reinforce their own skewed views.

While in the past locating details of long-hidden lore like that of Kundalini required substantial effort, today a simple online search reveals hundreds of postings on the subject, most of which merely parrot what is found on other sites. Kundalini thus becomes yet another victim of our self-referential “culture of screens”, trapped in a web of reflections made by observers who mostly mirror the echoes of others. This would be less a hindrance if Kundalini were a concrete substance or action, like an antique clock or an abdominal exercise; what makes this particularly problematic is that Kundalini is one’s power of self-identification.

Kundalini is the Shakti of individuation, of I-ness, our personal fragment of the cosmic power of self-identification, thanks to which we exist as individuals. So long as we are embodied we each bind ourselves to the world by adding to and subtracting from our personalities. This self-identifying power can however be termed “Kundalini” only when its focus rests from reinforcing who we are and endeavors to awaken us to what we might become. Until then this self-same power binds us more firmly to manifested reality, awake to the world and asleep to the Absolute. When manifesting in this fashion we term that power *ahamkara*, a word that means “the I-former”. The very same energy that ties us down to our limitations when acting as ahamkara, frees us from those limitations when serving as Kundalini. One’s personal existence as a human being is defined by the equilibrium that develops as ahamkara strives to move us further into manifestation and Kundalini seeks to move us away from materialization. Because the physical body provides an ever-present, exceedingly tangible object with which ahamkara can identify, ahamkara overwhelmingly predominates and Kundalini remains snoozing at the base of the spine in all but a very few individuals.

In the past ahamkara chiefly self-identified with the limited personality as tied down to the body that hosts it; today, as Internet use increases relentlessly around the globe, people disremember that they even have bodies as they focus instead on the likenesses of themselves that our devices generate and propagate. Since all of us are caught in each other’s projections and are defined in large part by them, billions of humans are now engaged in a colossal uncontrolled experiment in redefining ourselves into flatness. From robust three-dimensional living, breathing organisms we are being transformed into two-dimensional images in cyberspace, that artificial existence-sphere that has become for many the only domain in which they now interact with others.

One of the many hardships that loss of one dimension of reality generates is an escalation in the importance of the shallow at the expense of the deep, of the superficial at the expense of the heartfelt. This “hollowing out” of existence promotes the swiftly propagating “selfie” culture in which the electronic preservation of one’s own appearance has come to be for many the sole reason for performing any activity. Paradoxically, the possibilities that the Internet offers to free oneself from personal limitations too often generate instead a compulsion to bind oneself yet more firmly to a silicon doppelgänger co-created by whatever electronic “community” we may choose to haunt.

It is ironic that this human-created subtle realm that is cyberspace, which by its disconnecting us from material reality could assist us as a species to move toward the spiritual planes, chiefly serves instead to bind us more firmly to the transitory realities that form the most superficial portions of our personalities. It is yet more subtly ironic that the broad dissemination of the concept of Kundalini makes it easier for some, as ahamkara starts to release its hold on the Five Elements and the Kundalini energy within them begins to awaken, to fall prey to an urge to tie themselves firmly to the very process of this awakening. Such misidentification makes an idol of the concept of Kundalini when Kundalini’s sole purpose is to free ourselves of all idols, all limiting features of our current self-definition, that we may first perceive and eventually embody more fully the wide reality that lies beyond the narrowed self.

As Kundalini gains primacy over ahamkara, new and improved embodied realities become possible, each of which will be unique. From these transcendent perspectives innovative and enhanced perceptions of the nature of existence may arise, none of which need necessarily refer directly or even indirectly to Kundalini. Though to my knowledge Siddhartha Gautama, the Buddha, never spoke of Kundalini per se, his quadripartite expression of the nature of manifested existence offers a profound insight into the nature of the ahamkara-Kundalini energy as well:

*Sarvam kshanikam*

*Sarvam duhkham*

*Sarvam svalakshanam*

*Sarvam shunyam*

*Sarvam kshanikam*: Everything is Temporary

The Sanskrit noun *kshana* means “any instantaneous moment of time”. Every instant our energy of self-identification chooses what it will identify with; every moment we have the choice of remaining who we believe ourselves to be or opening ourselves to becoming someone new. Until we gain a state as exalted as that of the Buddha, we are neither permanently “unenlightened” or “enlightened”, for each split second offers the potential for a change of state.

*Sarvam duhkham*: Everything is Uneasy

Though the Sanskrit adjective *duhkha* is often translated as painful, miserable, troublesome or difficult, it literally means “inharmonious space”. Within the body of a living protoplasmic being, duhkha indicates that the various spaces of that organism are unable to permit *prana*, the life force, to move within it optimally. When prana cannot circulate properly then the healthy flow of life is interrupted, resulting in disturbance to body, mind and spirit that may culminate in evident disease. When prana moves well we are “in the flow” and there is a certain ease to existence, but it is in the nature of life here in *mrtyuloka* (the dimension of death) that flow is always being interrupted by urges like hunger and thirst, by desires, strong emotions, aches and pains, diseases, the deteriorations of old age, and death (friends’ and loved ones’, and ultimately our own). When we are in the flow, we experience life as *sukha*—happy, healthy, well-aligned, full of ease, but the reality of our lives is that, unlike duhkha, sukha develops only with persistent effort.

This applies particularly to the ahamkara-Kundalini equation, for an enlightened ahamkara usually proves far superior as a tool for creating and maintaining the integrity of one’s person than does an awakening Kundalini that has no direction or, worse, is directed toward an improper objective. For Kundalini the only “safe space” is *sushumna*, the central channel of the pranic body, and only a purified sushumna can offer Kundalini the space in which that Shakti can hope to ever find “ease”.

*Sarvam svalakshanam*: Everything has its Own Aim

Though the word *svalakshana* chiefly means a thing’s own specific characteristic or mark by which it can be recognized, the word *laksha* can also mean to aim at or direct towards. Ahamkara identifies with the individual’s inborn svalakshana, the physical and mental characteristics that make each of us who we are, in the particularized sense of our distinct personhood. As we change, so does our svalakshana. For Kundalini, svalakshana is the object toward which She is oriented, the target that She seeks. She expresses Her reality according to that laksha. As it changes, so does She.

*Sarvam shunyam*: Everything is Insignificant

This identical sentiment appears in *Ecclesiastes 1:2* (in the Bible’s King James Version):

*Vanity of vanities, saith the Preacher, vanity of vanities; all is vanity.*

In the time of King James “vanity” mostly meant “futility, emptiness, unreality.” Like all other humans who have ever been born we too will die and will ultimately be forgotten; in this sense we will live in vain. While we are alive ahamkara must believe sincerely in the importance of our person, otherwise it could not maintain our personhood amidst all the predators and parasites that endeavor to dine upon us. But to live a life that has some meaning, we must permit Kundalini to display to us the full extent of the unreality of our personhood, that we may to whatever extent discover a way to participate in that which is Real. Those who discover that our limited existence is ultimately void but who fail to detect the perennial Existence that lies behind it may sink into nihilism and conclude that the universe is indifferent to us and life is void of all meaning. But as Vimalananda was fond of saying, *shunyata*, the Void, only appears to be empty. It is in fact full—full of Reality. Only when Kundalini fully embraces that Void can one approach that Reality.

Alas, the screens that now monopolize our attention with their constantly fluctuating (*kshanikam*) forms and shrouded aims (svalakshana) are ultimately devoid (*shunyam*) of value and so can but provide us with unease (duhkha). How can it be otherwise when we seek to substitute these fake fires for fire itself? The human relationship with fire, which began one and one-half million years ago, has been utterly transformative for our species. Fire provided early hominids with light, warmth, protection from predators and insects, assistance in forming tools and, perhaps most importantly for our evolution, cooking, which dramatically widened their range of edible substances. Cooking also improves the digestibility of starchy foods and meat, destroys many naturally-occurring poisons, and kills parasites and food poisoning bacteria. Many scientists believe that it was this increase in the spectrum and digestibility of foods that spurred the human brain’s dramatic increase in size, which paved the way for our advanced intellectual development. Fire subsequently was adapted for signaling, for clearing vegetation from landscapes to make hunting easier and, after the commencement of agriculture, to clear land for planting and increase land fertility. Yet later it was harnessed for smelting and forging, to incinerate waste and cremate bodies, and for weapons and propulsion.

Fire is the very basis of Indian culture, being as it is the foundation of the Vedas; the first word of the first verse of the first hymn of the first *mandala* (division) of the *Rg Veda*, the first of the Vedas, is *Agni*, “fire”. But fire must have become central to ritual well before the Vedic era, for of the Five Great Elements (*mahabhuta*)—Earth, Water, Fire, Air and Space—Fire is the only one which must be brought to life and continuously fed and tended until it dies. Given the centrality of personhood to the human experience it would not have taken long for the Ancient Ones to discover that in addition to possessing its own personality as an “elemental” deity Fire can assist us to connect with other astral personalities. That first verse of the *Rg Veda* salutes Agni as the *purohita*, “he who is placed in front”, which in this context suggests a priest who serves as an intermediary between the terrestrial and the celestial worlds, facilitating for us the vision of deities within its very flames.

The Sanskrit word *darshana* means vision both literally (sight, seeing, and the act of seeing) and figuratively (philosophical system, doctrine, way of looking at the world). *Darshana* means yet more; a partial list of its denotations includes:

> looking at, noticing, sightseeing, the eye, anything seen, becoming visible or known, color, showing, showing a mirror, appearance, semblance, apprehension, observing, observation, inspection, experiencing, audience, aspect, exhibiting, being mentioned, knowing, teaching, examination, meeting, opinion, judgment, discernment, perception, understanding, contemplating, intellect, intention, foreseeing, dream, sacrifice

All these meanings are, ultimately, derived from that primary meaning of sight, the sense that is most important to us, so important that by some estimates up to 80% of all our sensory impressions are received via vision. In the Sankhya philosophy the subtle form (*tanmatra*) of the Fire Element is *rupa*, which means form (as also quality of color), and over the millennia, with the assistance of fire, the visual perception of form and color rose to the prominence that they enjoy today. I firmly believe that fire itself promoted the dramatic development of our visual cortex, and installed sight at the foundation of our world view. When it comes to projecting awareness into potential futures we do so with our vision: We imag(e)-ine, visual-ize, en-vision, en-visage. We never advise someone to smell-ize or taste-ize, nor do we en-touch, and we can at best occasionally, and limitedly, “sound something out”. We also communicate visually, via the form of speech known as *pashyanti*. In this very volume Vimalananda explains the spiritual use of pashyanti, but all of us use a lesser form of this profound language every time we actually ‘see’ what something means or when we gain an apparition of how a situation might play out.

It was the fire, external and internal (at the solar plexus), that taught us how to *see*, and for the many hundreds of thousands of years that every human alive lived day and night with open flame—as hundreds of millions still do—they were perpetually reminded to perceive accurately, both to keep the fire alive and to prevent it from getting loose and devouring what we don’t want burnt. Even in portions of the modern world it has been but decades since some people were obliged to build fires regularly. My Aunt Elenora would reminisce about how late into the 1940s before her portion of rural Texas was electrified, she and Uncle Troy would have to build a fire in the dairy barn on cold winter mornings before they started to milk the cows.

The continual shifting shapes and colors of open flame have long provided entertainment to observing bipeds, and it is ironically synchronistic that, just as actual flame was being banished from the last U.S. households, artificial flame in the form of television was barging into them. Movies had already been providing folks with shifting forms (rupa) as ersatz versions of authentic fire since the early 20th century, but one had to visit a cinema hall in order to be exposed to them. Television brought mesmerizing images directly into one’s home, first in black and white (my earliest idiot box memories are in greyscale) but soon enough in color, semblances soon sufficiently voluminous that they began to supplant personal thought processes. My mother liked to recount an episode from her time as a school teacher that became increasingly poignant for me as, with passing years, media multiplied. During the days when TV was gradually but inexorably supplanting radio as the home entertainment system of choice, she once asked her class which of the two they preferred. Most said TV, but one little girl said radio, and when asked why she replied, “I like the pictures better.”

The drawn, painted or graven images that have accompanied storytelling for many tens of thousands of years were always adjuncts, suggestions to assist listeners to illustrate the tales with their individual mental pictures. But with the relentless progression of cinema to boob tube to videos and now portable screens, commercial images have muscled aside and crowded out personal picturing. Most urbanites today would find it tough to conjure up within themselves any original image, so perfused are they with portrayals from elsewhere that insistently herd them toward their chosen self-imposed servitude.

No surprise then that our innate ability to at least periodically tap into pashyanti is swiftly atrophying, and with it our ability to separate fake from authentic, to detect the needle of truth in the haystack of falsity. The difference between screens and reality is the difference between artificial and genuine fire, and addiction to screens ruins all varieties of digestion: spiritual, moral, mental, physical, even financial. One symptom: a willingness to sink further and further into debt without ever thinking of how it might be repaid. Total world debt now totals hundreds of trillions (with a ‘t’) of dollars and continues to rise fast.

This is nothing new; more than two thousand years ago the Indian materialist Charvaka philosophy could fit snugly into this couplet:

> *yavat jivet, sukham jivet* *rnam krtva, ghrtam pibet*

“However long you live, you should live happily, so borrow money and drink ghee,” for according to the Charvakas this is the only world that exists, and any enjoyment can only happen here. Borrow as much as you can, live for pleasure alone, and then die; who can collect on your loans once you are dead? Note that from the perspective of Buddha noted above this definition of sukha is in fact much closer to a recipe for duhkha, for there is no unbridled pleasure that is not followed by some manner of pain. Most people for example find pleasure in food, and many people choose to overeat, with the result that today nearly one-third of the population of the world is either overweight or obese, including one of every five children in the USA. These children are guaranteed a life of duhkha for, as Ayurveda rightly noted many centuries back, “there is no medicine for obesity”, particularly not when obesity develops at tender age.

As there is now no escape from screens, particularly as online interaction with governments becomes increasingly obligatory around the world, salvation from enthrallment to our electronic overlords can only come individually, each of us choosing to reestablish a personal relationship with fire. While in a body we are always negotiating with the Five Great Elements, and Fire is the element that manages our equation with all five. We all need to return to negotiating directly with fire if we hope to return to any kind of alignment with reality.

Well before screens took us over Vimalananda felt the agony of the modern world, and his words are even more pertinent now as tragedy devolves into farce politically, environmentally, ethically, existentially. Over the nearly half-century since I first sat with Vimalananda for homa I have discovered that the best way to develop a healthy long-term relationship with fire is simply to get started: Build a fire, sit before it, align with it however you are able. Mistakes will be made; expect the occasional singe. Begin with the intention of creating an affiliation between your personality and that of fire, and as you feel that progressing, you may request the fire to progressively remove “you” from the equation, replacing “you” with the divine personality of your choice. Active cultivation of prana will assist both the maturation of your relationship (since you are the “fuel” for that internal fire and your prana is the “air” that keeps it enkindled) and the gradual freeing of Kundalini from her earthly shackles. Always remember that whatever you put into the fire will return to you multiplied a hundredfold, so if at some moment you happen to be overcome with some powerful passion like anger either make it a point of using your worship to incinerate that ire or wait until that moment has passed. Make your bond with fire a priority; dedicate yourself to that bond and sit with fire again and again and again.

Working from the template that Vimalananda provided me I have used my intuition to experiment and develop my own fire ceremony, assisted by valuable pointers gleaned from shamans, swamis and others who revere flame. My rapport with fire has expanded and deepened over the years, and when I have blundered it has kindly reproved me with the rare significant burn to show for my miscues. Except in circumstances when specific results were desired, I have, when sitting for homa, always pictured myself making offerings to a burning funeral pyre in a smashan, where all, embodied or ethereal, are welcome to participate so long as they behave. This has assisted me to develop relationships with beings of all sorts all over the world, to my benefit and, so far as I can discern, to theirs as well.

As your alliance develops, your sushumna will become purified and opened, since sushumna is itself of the nature of fire. The clearer the path that sushumna offers for Kundalini, the better the results will be. Even after more than forty years of fire worship I continue to discover new things, including just how much I have yet to learn. Even if I cannot presume in this lifetime ever to achieve the degree of alignment that Guru Maharaj had achieved with his fire, that relationship, a token of which I witnessed in person one rainy day, remains an inspiration to me, offers me something to aspire to.

It was a typical monsoon morning there at his ashram, occasional showers punctuated by relentless drizzle, and as always Guru Maharaj had sat for more than an hour doing homa at his *dhuni*, his personal fire, after which as usual he covered the fire with green boughs and let it smoke. A couple of hours had passed since he had arisen thence, and he and I were standing in discussion in a doorway ten or fifteen meters away as the previous sprinkle enthusiastically accelerated. When I turned for a moment to look at the dhuni I was startled to see a strong, sharp, narrow flame more than a meter in height erupting vertically from it. As I turned to Guru Maharaj with inquiring eyes he said simply, “When you have a dhuni, it should always reflect whatever is going on inside you.”

Guru Maharaj had had of course more than four centuries to perfect his art of the blaze, and his diet of milk, soda pop and tobacco undoubtedly contributed to the precision and lucidity with which he and his dhuni communed. But what must the state of his sushumna have been at that moment, to have so dramatically reawakened a few coals buried for hours under waterlogged leafy branches in ongoing precipitation? And what sort of “mirror neurons” must his dhuni have possessed for it to respond so accurately?

That day my own mirror neurons served me well, for when he spoke those words I could feel within myself, albeit faintly, the truth of what he meant, and that one feeling was worth a thousand instructions. My own Kundalini stood a little straighter in the presence of his, I gained a precise perspective on just how to align with Fire and just how aligned with Fire a human can become, and I was gifted direct insight into why sushumna is said to be of the nature of Fire.

My sadhana always took “quantum” leaps via this mechanism whenever Guru Maharaj would permit me a peek into some facet of his experience. Via Vimalananda I already had familiarity with some of the ways that a potent individual can actively control external reality, but on this occasion I could perceive just how different Guru Maharaj was from the run-of-the-samsara human. Presumably there had been a time in his very early life that Guru Maharaj was affected by fire just like normal people are, but then he developed such a profound relationship with that element that he began to affect it, and so strongly that, as in this instance, the fire responded to him by exceeding its own limits (as no ordinary fire built of small sticks could have ever produced such a flame, much less done so after being covered and doused). By communicating his personal reality to the Fire Element he induced it evolve, thus becoming a guru to Fire itself!

Rarely though would Guru Maharaj permit such a window into his inner reality open to the observation of others; most of the time he guarded himself too well to let anything significant slip out. With Vimalananda it was different; he was always experimenting with something, and, while sitting with him, all manner of spontaneous insights would come my way when I was able to align with him at the many moments that he was “in the zone”. Whether or not I could literally *see* what he meant or was doing, I could frequently *feel* it, sometimes by a process of active “conductivity” and sometimes via passive “lack of resistance” to his field of reality. It was an aligning of auras, a communication of charisma, as my organism “shadowed” his in a demonstration of the practical value of spending serious time in the physical presence of a teacher.

When with him I often found that I could perform difficult tasks better than I could perform them when on my own. This enabled me to make better progress then and benefited me later when, studying with experts in subjects like yoga and Jyotisha, I found a similar process operating, finding myself able to do things when in their presence that I couldn’t do on my own. And I have no doubt that repeatedly experiencing Vimalananda’s states of possession facilitated my own limited experiences of possession during the time I was with him, and the many and more substantial experiences I have had since.

Of course, there were also many things that Vimalananda could do that I could never “tune into”, many things that I still cannot comprehend, much less feel in myself, as on that afternoon when, cigarette in hand and in a mood of great concentration, he exhaled a single perfectly cylindrical thin stream of smoke from one nostril. Like a good sadhu at his dhuni, Vimalananda’s body responded to the condition of his Kundalini, which he regularly used to test the condition of my own Kundalini, testing which itself helped me cultivate my Kundalini. With me this was his chief teaching method; rarely would he offer specific directions, though often he would offer hints, e.g. how to get ideas about what sort of being a person might have been in his or her previous incarnation. I had lots of practice visualizing realities as he told stories, and plenty of opportunities to winnow out the “alternative facts” that he would offer, for whatever reason, to his audiences. He never insisted that I believe him, partly because he freely exaggerated, and perhaps as well because he knew that reality was being transmitted whether I could perceive it or not.

Early on he made it clear that, though I would eventually meet my guru in this lifetime, neither he nor Guru Maharaj was that guru. He advised me that until that time I should remain constantly alert to the teachings that Nature is always providing, citing the example of Dattatreya, none of whose twenty-four gurus were aware that they were teaching him. This is exceptionally good advice nowadays, when genuine gurus are so hard to find. If, like me, you have yet to meet your actual guru, don’t waste your time while you wait for him or her to arrive. Learn from Nature, prepare yourself with *niyamas* (good habits): Learn to sit calmly, treat your body like your deity’s temple, be of service to others, and always chant the sweet name of God.

Beware though of the self-administration of intoxicants, which many aspirants attempt to use as Vimalananda did for self-cultivation. Intoxicants are also a type of fire, one that illuminates by temporarily incinerating our calcified world-views, but in the absence of a reliable teacher who has stabilized his/her body/mind in the context of these substances, their use is more likely to destabilize than to benefit you. With intoxicants guidance by an expert is crucial, and a clear focus on a worthy goal is a must, for positive results to occur.

Whether or not your karmas are such that your Kundalini draws you in to work with Her directly, learning about Her via the mirroring process will assist Her to work with you. Never try to guide Her to behave as you fancy She ought to behave; always permit Her to lead while you maintain strong focus on your goal. Work with Her slowly, gradually and respectfully as She shifts from existing for you as a mere concept that you employ into a reality that will employ you, hopefully for your greater good and that of all sentient beings. May She be merciful to us all!

Aghora: Book II: Kundalini

**INTRODUCTION TO THE FIRST EDITION**

The study of philosophy without a longing for liberation is like dressing up a corpse.

*Tripura Rahasya*

IN EARLIER TIMES, WHEN ESOTERIC KNOWLEDGE was under jealous guard, a spiritual aspirant usually had to endure years of patient waiting before being taught. Now that information has become an article of commerce, all manner of secrets would seem to have become available for purchase—though what can be so easily possessed will not necessarily be so easily comprehended. Words may be bought and sold, but living wisdom, which cannot be confined within words, must still be earned.

Among the long-hidden arcana now being packaged for sale is the lore of Kundalini, and most writers who try to present to the world this living knowledge produce only dead words. As Heinrich Zimmer observed, “The best things can’t be told; the second best are misunderstood.” Carl Jung, who many decades ago delivered a series of lectures on Kundalini, explains why:

> [T]he Yoga way or the Yoga philosophy has always been a secret, but not because people have kept it secret. For as soon as you keep a secret it is already an open secret: you know about it and other people know about it, and then it is no longer a secret. The real secrets are secrets because no one understands them. One cannot even talk about them, and of such a kind are the experiences of Kundalini Yoga. That tendency to keep things secret is merely a natural consequence when the experience is of such a peculiar kind that you had better not talk about it, for you would expose yourself to the greatest misunderstanding and misinterpretation.1

The experiences of Kundalini Yoga are peculiar because Kundalini is the source of all individual experiences. Kundalini is that in-dwelling energy which by self-identifying with your opinions and character traits accretes and preserves your identity. In Jung’s words:

> … according to the Tantric teaching, there is an urge to produce a personality, something that is centered, and divided from other beings. … It is what one would describe in Western philosophical terms as an urge or instinct toward individuation. The instinct of individuation is found everywhere in life, for there is no life on earth that is not individual. Individuation takes place only when you are conscious of it, but individuality is always there from the beginning of your existence. (Jung, p. 2)

So long as the urge toward individuation is mainly directed toward benefiting your own limited temporary individual self it is called *ahamkara*, or egoism, the force which makes it possible for you to unquestioningly accept the world as it is on the surface. This same force is called *Kundalini* when it turns away from the mundane and toward the spiritual, the permanent and eternal. After Kundalini awakes it becomes impossible to continue believing that external reality is the sole reality. Ahamkara makes you who you are now; Kundalini makes you into what you will become.

Kundalini has remained secret for so long because, as Jung notes, it cannot be understood; it can only be experienced. The process of spiritual evolution cannot be objectified and separated from the subject who evolves, for Kundalini functions simultaneously as descriptive consciousness, as the thing described, and as its description. Since human language is made up of subjects and objects, descriptions of Kundalini tend to be skewed either toward objective comment on the experience, which devitalizes it, or toward description of the raw subjective experience itself, which is usually distorted by the experiencer’s mental imbalances, stresses and fantasies.

Among the writers who have made valuable contributions to the literature on Kundalini are Sir John Woodroffe (Arthur Avalon), an Englishman who was initiated into Tantra while serving as a judge in India, and Gopi Krishna, a Kashmiri Pandit who suffered terrifying consequences when his own Kundalini was awakened before he knew how to deal with it. While neither perfectly conveys Kundalini’s incomprehensible secrets, since their words get in their way, here and there in inspired passages Kundalini’s radiance flashes momentarily through, like lightning through a somber sky.

These accounts succeed, albeit partially, because their information has not been lifted out of context. Though Kundalini as Shakti is omnipresent, Kundalini as concept can be understood solely within the context of Indian culture. Ever since the time of the early Theosophists, though, most Western interpreters of Kundalini, in order to import into their own systems of psychology concepts which they believe to be Tantric, have unfortunately not hesitated to assign to Tantric words denotations which often vary significantly from their original meanings.

Jung himself borrowed concepts from Kundalini Yoga, including the very concept of Kundalini, which he called the anima, and so he bears some of the blame for this situation. At least he was more forthright than are most distorters of Kundalini:

> One needs a great deal of psychology in order to make these matters palatable to the Western mind, and unless we try hard and dare to commit many errors in assimilating it to our Western mentality, we simply get poisoned. For these symbols have a terribly clinging tendency. They catch the unconscious somehow and cling to us. But they are a foreign body in our system—*corpus alienum*—and they inhibit the natural growth and development of our own psychology. It is like a secondary growth or poison. Therefore one has to make really heroic attempts to master these things, to stand up against these symbols, in order to deprive them of their influence. Perhaps you cannot fully realize what I say, but take it as a hypothesis – though it is more than a hypothesis. It is a truth. I have seen too often how dangerous their influence may be. (Jung, p. 9)

Rejecting those concepts that “we do not need” for a systematic psychological description of Western experiences with the unconscious, Jung rationalized:

> We can only understand their picture of the world in as much as we try to understand it in our own terms. Therefore I make the attempt to approach it from the psychological point of view. I am sorry to have bewildered you, but you will be more bewildered if you take these things literally (you had better not). If you think in these terms, you will build up an apparent Hindu system with the psychology of the Western mind, and you cannot do that. You simply poison yourself! (Jung, p. 13)

Possibly those who try hard and dare to commit many errors to assimilate concepts from Kundalini Yoga into popular psychology do avoid the fate of the many Westerners who have poisoned themselves by dressing their minds in Indian vestments. But while replicas of Kundalini Yoga may function well enough in the external world of consensus reality to be useful psychological tools, they cannot substitute for the real thing when it comes to spiritual development. This is particularly true for those people who, by design or by accident, have broken through some of the barriers which separate objective from subjective reality and live lives in which waking reality and symbolic reality compete with one another for attention. Such individuals risk being trapped on an unknown ocean in a leaky conceptual boat if they try to rely on psychology alone to carry them safely to shore.

An awakening into the reality of the nonphysical in a person who lacks adequate prior preparation usually precipitates a personal crisis; such people may seem crazy, are often thought to be crazy, and sometimes believe themselves to be going crazy, all because they can no longer unquestioningly accept our “standard” reality. Most of those who lose touch with everyday reality are in fact insane, of course, but in a sizable number of cases the cause is a spiritual crisis.

The prophet Ezekiel once heard a divine voice command him to sleep on his right side for 390 nights and then to switch to his left side for 40 more. (Ezekiel 4:4-6) Yogis know that the position in which you sleep exerts a profound effect on your physiology, and so your consciousness; most psychologists do not, and would conclude that Ezekiel and others like him who heard voices (St. Teresa of Avila, for example), were schizophrenic, when in fact they were being inspired by a reality of which science know next to nothing.

A spiritual awakening alters forever the way in which an individual experiences the world, for after the initial crisis abates one discovers that there is no way to return to one’s previously comfortable mindset. Once aroused and unboxed Kundalini is not “derousable”; the genie will not fit back into the bottle. “After the awakening, the devotee lives always at the mercy of Kundalini,”2 says Pandit Gopi Krishna, who experienced several crises during which the speed, insouciance and authority of the power he had unleashed terrified him. That power which caused his terror, which he had to face without the help of any guide, can terrify or incapacitate anyone who awakens Kundalini without proper guidance.

So long as Kundalini remains within the realm of psychology, our relative objectivity can shield us from the influence of symbolic existence. Once we enter subjective reality, however, that realm in which symbols “cling,” we are at their mercy unless we have been taught how to deal with them. Those who ride Kundalini without knowing their destination risk losing their way. The result may be “ego inflation,” which occurs when one’s limited personality survives the crisis intact and the individual then “claims the lustre of the archetypal world for his or her own person,” or “ego deflation,” if the awakening thoroughly disrupts one’s self-integration and garbles one’s self image.

The savants of India have for thousands of years worked to perfect user-friendly methods of spiritual advancement that, when properly implemented, prepare individuals for and guide them through the process of individuation without terrorizing them. Each of these methods arouses the evolutionary power inherent in every individual, but this power appears as Kundalini in one system alone: the Tantric tradition. Anyone who wants to understand Kundalini as Kundalini must first come to grips with Tantra.

Though it has for centuries been maligned by the orthodox and puritanical among Indians, Tantra is not a religion of sensory indulgence which teaches the instant gratification of one’s cravings, nor is it a system for gaining control of others via magic. A good Tantrika believes in truth and reality, and in the facing of facts, the first of which is the fact that all of us are part of the manifested universe, subject to its laws until we develop the power to redefine ourselves in other terms. A Tantrika aims to become *sva-tantra* (“self-functioning”), to be free of all limitations, including especially the limitations of his or her own personality.

Tantra is not a subject one can learn in from books, because Tantra is not bookish knowledge; it is living wisdom which must be obtained directly from an experienced practitioner. A good guide, a guru who has already followed the path and knows all its pitfalls, is essential if one hopes to follow the Tantric path and arouse Kundalini without calamity; a powerless or ignorant guru is worse than none at all.

One such expert, the Aghori Vimalananda, taught me what I know of Kundalini. His uniquely original way of perceiving the world developed thanks to the awakening of his own Kundalini through a midnight ritual on a corpse. When Kundalini awakened for him, she took the form of the Tantric goddess Smashan Tara, the divinity of the cremation ground (*smashan*), who enables one to cross over from the reality of embodied life to the reality of disembodied existence. Smashan Tara, the “Savioress of the Cemetery,” enabled Vimalananda to navigate the voyage from his ordinary consciousness into states in which he could perceive reality from a different vantage. Many of his experiences at the time of this awakening and thereafter are recounted in my book *Aghora I: At the Left Hand of God*, describing the paths he followed in his odyssey from the darkness of unexamined mundane existence into the light of the celestial realms, all the while maintaining consciousness of both.

Aghora is a sort of super-Tantra, a Tantra in which all sense of limitation is removed. Aghora is the Path of the Shadow, the “shadow” being all those aspects of our lives that permit us to exist as individuals at the expense of other beings. We can know the light of altruism only because we have known the darkness of selfishness; only after passing through the Valley of the Shadow of Death do we learn how to live. Aghoris are psyche explorers who go down into the blackness of their individual conceits to find their way to true freedom. Their spiritual path is no anemic “sweetness and light” experience; an aghori must be “as hard as diamond and as soft as wax” as the situation demands. Only after the grapes of your ego-attachments have been thoroughly trodden into juice can you vint the sweet wine of spiritual wisdom.

Aghoris play the game of life with the utmost sobriety, fully aware of the wagers staked. No means to awakening is too disgusting or frightening for them, for they worship Death, the Great Transformer. *Aghora* literally means “non-terrifying”; an aghori takes the most terrifying experiences possible and transmutes them into devotion to Reality. Tradition sends aghoris to seek God in the cremation grounds, where death is ever-present, but a good aghori sees the entire world as one vast ongoing cremation. Aghoris personify and deify death, selecting one face of this Universal Reality as their Beloved and worshiping this deity with an intense and all-consuming love. Every day for Vimalananda was a day of play with the cosmos, his Lover, and he never tired of playing the games that lovers play, for those games brought him ever closer to his sweetheart.

When the goddess Kundalini awoke in him She had a form and a personality that he could interact with, which is likely why Kundalini spared Vimalananda the sort of anguish that She awarded Pandit Gopi Krishna. Had Panditji3 perhaps concentrated on a god or goddess instead of a lotus he too might have found a haven in which to rest when the tempest tossed him. To Vimalananda Kundalini was not a wild unapproachable force that batted him about according to Her whim; She was instead his Beloved Mother, in whose lap he sat, allowing Her to protect him from all dangers with Her overwhelming clout.

Vimalananda always preferred the path of worship of God-with-form to that of worship of the Impersonal Absolute. To him, the highest expression of divinity is the Motherhood of God, the God who protects and loves Her children no matter what errors they may make. This attitude was to him the best of all possible attitudes when dealing with Kundalini, because once you enter such a mother-child relationship all fear of damage by the energy disappears. Also, as he liked to say, “Bhakti is Shakti”: the energy (*Shakti*) that you put into devotion (*bhakti*) toward your chosen form of God is returned to you manyfold, benevolently amplified by the universe. As your devotion grows, so does your own personal power, which you are less likely to misuse since all you can think about is the One you love.

An aghori’s ache for a vision of the Beloved is so fierce that no means to achieve it is too extreme. This divine fury, a sort of cosmic thrill-seeking, is Aghora’s hallmark. ‘’Aghoris always overdo a thing,” as Vimalananda liked to say, and *Aghora I* documents how he frequently overdid things in his life. Overcome by his craving for his Beloved he worshiped with every element and substance and hobnobbed with every sort of ethereal being, all his rituals becoming, by Tantric transmutation, offerings to the divine.

Aghora’s field of activity is not limited to enthralling or repugnant practices. The path of Aghora is the path of spontaneity; every action must be appropriate to the context in which it is performed and must be performed at the moment most appropriate for its performance. Worship is worship to an aghori, in temple or charnel ground; with the surrender of all self interest except that single-minded quest to achieve the Beloved, an aghori can accept with love and thankfulness everything that God offers, bliss and misery alike, and transmute every experience, even a trip to the toilet, into an act of worship of the Absolute. Everything an aghori touches, desired and despised, clean and unclean, he drags from the periphery of his experience into the purity of his center to help develop the “critical mass” needed to ignite and sustain a “spiritual chain reaction.”

Vimalananda was a man of action, both “right-handed” and “left handed,” and cared little for scholarly views on what might or might not be classifiable as Aghora. He embraced accepted doctrine when it suited his purposes, while always retaining the right to innovate at any moment when necessity demanded. Over the ages philosophical systems have come and gone in India, but the spiritual springs from which they have sprung have continued to overflow. Indian spiritual tradition, Tantra particularly, has always ebbed and flowed between the twin shores of theory and practice. Theory perpetually regulates practice, and practice inexorably modifies theory. As fast as theologians erect and legitimize mountains of dogma, iconoclasts weather them down with their own individual interpretations; the heresies of yesterday are the orthodoxies of today.

Contemporary India is filled with individuals and sects working to legitimize their own unique aggregate of philosophy, cosmology and technique by assimilating their systems to the mainstream of “Vedic tradition” or “Kashmir Shaivism” or whatever, while other individuals and sects—often paradoxically the same ones—move away from such standardized definitions of religion, calling them convenient fictions that limit and mislead. Maintaining that modern circumstances (time, place, people) are too different to permit precise recovery or revival of the ancient ways, they assert that what must be revived within a system is the flame which gives it life, not its external form.

For his part Vimalananda cheerfully combined many seemingly contradictory theories and practices into “his” Aghora, quoting in his support the ancient text which taught that only bewildered people dispute about truth, for “what proposition is there that the learned cannot defend?” Though highly educated Vimalananda’s knowledge of Sanskrit texts was modest, and he neither knew nor cared to know much of the Tantric literature in Gujarati, Hindi or English translation, languages in which he was fluent. His textbook was life itself, and he could read from it meanings which are accessible only to those who know the secret language of spirituality.

While he called himself an aghori, Vimalananda’s disdain for organized religion distanced him from all recognized Aghora lineages, nor did he refer to his own mentors as aghoris (and it is doubtful that they would describe themselves thus). He preferred to follow his own path:

> I have never believed in religion. Religions are all limited because they concentrate only on one aspect of truth. That is why they are always fighting amongst one another, because they all think they are in sole possession of the truth. But I say there is no end to knowledge, so there is no use in trying to confine it to one scripture or one holy book or one experience. This is why, when people ask what religion I follow, I say, ‘I don’t believe in *sampradaya* (sect), I believe in *sampradaha* (incineration)’: Burn down everything that is getting in the way of your perception of truth. (*Aghora I*)

In this at least he agreed with Jung, who once observed that “the function of religion is to protect us from an experience of God.” The Aghori Vimalananda was too much the iconoclast and too determined to perceive reality to be imprisoned within dogma; his Aghora is a religion of consciousness, its precepts engraved not on tablets of stone but on the heart of the individual practitioner who must use them to create an individual system, thereby carving out his or her own spiritual niche.

There are not now and there never have been large numbers of aghoris abroad in the world, nor did Vimalananda create many. He was quick to tell most people, “Do as I say, not as I do,” and never permitted anyone to slavishly imitate him, because only those fit to dine on human brain will derive spiritual advantage from such a diet. He never devised any system of spiritual practices for the world to admire and follow. After carefully evaluating each individual who came to him he would teach certain lessons, directly or obliquely, or he would teach nothing at all, depending on his perception of his karmic connection with that person and his or her fitness and aptitude for spiritual disciplines. He never hesitated to challenge anyone’s assumptions on spirituality and yogic discipline and was not afraid to step on toes if he thought that he might arouse someone from their slumber by doing so. Vimalananda wanted his views to be spread to anyone willing to listen because he felt acutely the anguish of the emptiness of the modern world, whose god is Mammon and whose predominant religion is an arrogantly emotionless science which seems bent on suppressing what humanity remains within us. As society disintegrates and meaning dissolves from life, people tend either to descend into despair or to return to their roots. We in the West have for years been cutting ourselves off from our roots, and now, nearly rootless, we are slowly dying from lack of cultural nourishment.

Some Westerners seek to live without roots, hydroponically, through futurism, while others try to reinvent the past via the “men’s movement,” Goddess Worship, Afrocentrism and the like. Yet others search for roots in such still-living cultures as the Indian, Native American, Tibetan or Chinese, as if perhaps by donning their visages they can somehow assimilate their ways. We have, however, become so superficial that few of us know how to dive deep enough into the cultures we seek to emulate to tap into their roots, and so we usually, as Jung feared, poison ourselves.

Vimalananda had no more faith that mass spiritual movements can save us than he trusted in social programs, political activism or enforced morality to rescue us, since all such solutions are superficial; they change our clothing, not our inner beings. He believed that real change can come only through those individuals who are brave enough to examine their assumptions about reality and change those which must be changed. The numerous misconceptions about spirituality which permeate our modem world make his teachings on Kundalini valuable for everyone trying to follow a spiritual path. Before his death Vimalananda charged me with the responsibility of presenting his musings to those willing to listen, as much to organize my knowledge, refine my understanding and manifest my creativity as to instruct others. He also wanted me to have something solid to remember him by, so that whenever I want to be with him again I need only open the book.

Like other great teachers Vimalananda had a knack for being right, as well as an outstanding ability to impart his knowledge to those around him, usually when they least expected it, via an uncommon perspective, mixing theory with anecdote or letting anecdote reveal theory. His urbanity and ready humor betrayed a virtuosity in his play with the world which often masked an extraordinary shrewdness. He was never shy to speak out, and at times he seems as arrogant, harsh, critical, opinionated, pretentious and ready with stereotypes as much as at others his words are filled with sweetness, compassion, love, devotion, farsightedness and attention to detail. In his opinions and often peculiar views he was unequivocal and unshakable, which earned him both fear and respect, and a reputation variously as genius, crank, wise man and man of God. Whatever else he may have been, Vimalananda was always real, always true to himself and his vision of reality, and he encouraged anyone who couldn’t stand his heat to leave his kitchen.

Those who are convinced that the real is limited to that which our senses can perceive have asked, “Did all the events described by Vimalananda actually ‘occur’ in the outward sense of our consensus reality, or did they occur solely within his individual consciousness?” Better they should ask this question of themselves, for few people realize just how much of their reality is manipulated. Media today synthesizes miraculous phenomena for our amazement, manipulating our emotions and blurring the line between what is ‘real’ and what is fake. We are already on the cusp of a time when we will literally be unable to believe our eyes and ears, when soon we will not dare to rely any longer on the ‘objective’ truth of our sensory perceptions.

One of the chief arguments against “subjective” reality is that since it has no physical substance it is ephemeral. This is certainly true, at least on the surface (which is where objective reality operates, after all). But below the surface it is objective reality which is found to be impermanent, while the reality of ideas, memories and reflections goes on and on. This truth is brought home to me regularly in India, where for instance people, when giving directions, often refer to landmarks which were demolished years before. Clearly, a structure’s ethereal reality may persist long after its physical form has disappeared.

Though all living beings eventually lose their physical existence, they continue to live on in the hearts of those who remember them. Some live on in memory for generations; a few, like Jesus, will live and continue to transform human lives forever. Others, like the gods and goddesses of India, are remembered even though they may never have lived in physical bodies. Are they real? We in India believe that they are far more real than humans are, even though their existence may not be provable by methods acceptable to materialistic science.

Whether these acts that Vimalananda recounts actually ‘happened’ in the physical world or not is impossible to say, for India is truly a mysterious country and strange things do happen there. Many people who knew Vimalananda experienced many unusual things when in his vicinity, and most of them attributed such occurrences to him, while he attributed them all to the Great Goddess: “I am talking as if I do these things, but in fact it is beyond me to do anything. Only Ma can do it; She does it all. This is the foundation of all my confidence in my abilities. Do I have any capabilities? Ha! Everything is from Ma.” Vimalananda was an artist of consciousness and it is enough to know that his experiences were intensely real and true for him, and that they can be real and true for anyone who is open to the possibility of their being so. Every time I reread an incident from his life it suddenly becomes real for me again, no matter how long ago it transpired, and Vimalananda thus continues to live for me.

Vimalananda was convinced by his experiments, as some modern physicists have become convinced by their experiments, that it is impossible to speak of objective reality without taking the assistance of a subjective observer, a ‘knower’ whose observation irrevocably alters the reality thus observed. That shift in perception which allows aghoris to know that knower, known and the process of knowing are one and the same Reality allows them to perceive miraculous phenomena, whether obvious to others or not. Aghoris control their thoughts and emotions themselves that they may better enjoy the reality they so crave: the company of the Beloved.

Since human consciousness requires objects, this book speaks of Kundalini as if She can be considered in isolation from the individual in whom She exists. Kundalini cannot ultimately be objectified, but until She is awakened in an individual She exists for him or her only as a concept, and so She can be relatively objectified. As She awakens, this relative objectification is progressively converted into relative subjectification, until when Kundalini has been completely aroused one moves wholly into subjective consciousness, and descriptions lose their utility.

This is one of the reasons that Vimalananda preferred to deal with people individually, and never discoursed in public. When he met someone, he would use words to express the objective portion of what he had to communicate and would express the subjective part by other means. Since each individual’s consciousness is made up of a particular ratio of subjective to objective awareness, the targeted individual would often get the message while other people in the room might miss it entirely. I have tried to select from the many objective messages directed at me over the years those which are sufficiently general to be of use to readers who knew neither of us, and I have tried to compile them in such a way as to permit some of his subjective messages to come through as well.

Vimalananda insisted on anonymity in these books because the less people know of the external details of his life the less they will be distracted from looking at what he wants them to see: the internal details. Anonymity protects his message—the subjective story of his existence—from any nitpicking over its objective details. A consummate actor, he compiled his teachings in the book of his life, in actions rather than in words, his every action a well-thought-out statement in the chapters of his saga. Though he insisted on anonymity both for himself and for his mentors, the name Vimalananda is not wholly pseudonymous; he used it occasionally in his younger days. It means both “son of Vimala” (his mother’s name was Vimala) and “he whose bliss is stainless.” Through all the ups and downs of his life, in days of anguish or exaltation, Vimalananda’s bliss was ever stainless, because he was a true aghori, perpetually intoxicated with love for God.

As I struggled over the writing of *Aghora I*, I realized that no single angle provides a truly accurate view of Vimalananda, and so I decided to use three angles. In *Aghora I* Vimalananda tells his own story subjectively, presenting his life and work in his own words much as they were spoken to me, his scribe. In this book he appears much as I saw and objectified him, and in the third volume will appear the artist that was Vimalananda, constantly at work in the atelier of the world around him.

This book deals predominantly with Vimalananda’s approach to the details of Tantra, as outlined in the preface to *Aghora I*, and has three protagonists: the teacher (Vimalananda), the taught (me), and the teaching. Most of it is dialogue, or rather trialogue, since the teaching has its own voice. Living wisdom cannot be confined within words, but it can be hinted at through situations, much as a specific feature of an otherwise undistinguished landscape can often be discerned by following the path projected by a pointing finger. “Whoever has ears, let them hear,” said Jesus (Matthew 11:15); whoever ‘hears’ the inner import of Vimalananda’s words will be able to ‘see’ their inward meaning. So read on, and listen!

1 Jung, Carl, “Psychological Commentaries on Kundalini Yoga, Lectures One and Two—1932”, Spring 1975, Spring Publications, New York, p.20.

2 Krishna, Gopi, Kundalini: *The Evolutionary Energy in Man*, Boulder: Shambala Publications, 1971.

3 Ji” when appended to the end of a name or title is a form of respect in many Indian languages, particularly Hindi.

Aghora: Book II: Kundalini

**CHAPTER ONE**

**AGNI**

WHEN IN SEPTEMBER 1975 I MET VIMALANANDA I had already been living in the city of Poona1 for more than a year, studying Ayurveda, India’s ancient medical system, and yoga. I had spiritual ambitions but despite guidance from saintly personages I had no real idea of my ultimate spiritual aim. Still, I dutifully invested many hours each day in postures and breathing exercises, holy books, incense and meditation while I waited impatiently for something to happen. To help pay for my schooling I had competed for and won a Youthgrant from the National Endowment for the Humanities to report on how Ayurveda was being practiced in Poona. For this purpose I interviewed physicians of all sorts in the area, and one day my Sanskrit professor, who had been helping me locate interviewees, announced that he had located a most unusual Ayurvedic physician who was also a Tantrika as well as a race horse owner, someone who deserved to be met.

I greeted this news warily, as I had been cautioned of the great dangers inherent in Tantra. But since my mission was to obtain a cross section of healers and therapists, certainly I needed at least one Tantrika, since many of the Ayurvedic remedies in common use today have been derived directly from Tantric alchemy, and Tantra like Ayurveda has its roots in the Atharva Veda.

Vimalananda was at that time staying with a family in an Irani colony just a few blocks from my own residence. The first time I went to meet him he had gone to the racecourse, where an elderly mare he had recently purchased was running. When I retraced my steps to meet him later in the day I found the flush of victory suffusing his broad and handsome face.

I remember very little of that meeting, other than that when I mentioned a questionnaire that I wanted to use on him he suggested we talk about it on the following day. When I discovered on reflection that he had answered all my questions without my even asking them, I was impressed enough to return eagerly the next day, and the next. During these first days he predicted (correctly, as it turned out) that none of his immediate family would attend his funeral, and that I would cremate him. Such a prospect seemed almost as strange as his prediction of it, but he had taken me as his foster son almost from the moment we met, and it was soon natural enough to see him as friend instead of informant, and then to accept the proffered role of offspring.

Soon I was a frequent visitor to the Irani colony. These Iranis are a sect of Zoroastrian fire worshipers who exited Iran more recently than did their brethren the Parsis, who have lived in India for more than thirteen centuries. Soon I, like Vimalananda, became an auxiliary family member, tutoring at his behest the family’s younger daughter and spending many pleasant, quiet hours chatting with him when he made the trip up from Bombay to attend the races.

I had known him for only a week or two when he invited me to accompany him outside Poona on the occasion of a lunar eclipse for me to see, as he said, “how I do my rituals.” A month earlier I would have attended as a skeptical observer; now I accepted with alacrity, unsure of what I would find but certain that this was the right course of action for me.

We left Poona one morning by auto rickshaw, a motorized three-wheeled taxi, and a jolting three-quarters of an hour later we reached Alandi, our first stop. Alandi is the home of Jnaneshwar Maharaj, who lived there more than seven hundred years ago with his brothers and sister. (A guru is often called *maharaj*, “great king,” because he has become master of himself, and because he is the disciple’s absolute master.)

Jnaneshwar translated the *Bhagavad Gita* from Sanskrit into Marathi, the local language, and composed a commentary for it to make its teachings accessible to the common people. His commentary, properly titled the *Bhavarthdipika*, is known to all as the *Jnaneshwari*. It is the most beloved book in the state of Maharashtra, and his story is known to every Maharashtrian.

Jnaneshwar, who is renowned for making a buffalo speak the Vedas and causing a wall to fly in the air, tired of the world in his early twenties and took *jivana samadhi* (“living trance”): He entered a cave and had its entrance sealed behind him so that he could continue to meditate without interruption. His resting place was forgotten until a few hundred years later when another famous saint of Maharashtra, Eknath Maharaj, had a dream in which Jnaneshwar begged him to come to Alandi and save him from the tree above his head whose roots had begun to grow around his neck. Eknath Maharaj located Alandi, had a vision of Jnaneshwar who showed him where to look for the cave, found Jnaneshwar Maharaj, and removed the roots. Before resealing the cave, Eknath Maharaj took with him the manuscript of the *Jnaneshwari*, which was resting on Jnaneshwar’s lap. It is thus thanks to Eknath Maharaj that the *Jnaneshwari*, which had been inaccessible until that time, again saw the light of day.

A shrine now stands atop Jnaneshwar, and throngs of people come there regularly to worship God and to request Jnaneshwar’s assistance in solving their problems. Hundreds of pilgrims crowd the shrine each morning, making the rounds of each tree and image and offering their respects to the black stone beneath which he is said still to be sitting. In 1974 a group of ‘rationalists’ demanded that the government excavate the area beneath the shrine to determine if indeed Jnaneshwar continues to be resident there in the flesh. The ensuing public outcry prevented this from occurring, and Jnaneshwar sits there yet, enjoying the mingling of the devotional singing of the pilgrims who concentrate in his front courtyard with the murmuring of other penitents who recite the *Jnaneshwari* under the slim branches of the tree whose roots were the cause of its reappearance.

We sat at Jnaneshwar’s stone that morning with other members of our party for over an hour, together ritually requesting his assistance for our own more individualized worship scheduled for later that night. After similarly saluting Siddheshwar Mahadev, the Shiva Linga (phallic symbol of Shiva, the god of death and transformation) in the temple adjoining Jnaneshwar’s, we all repaired to our officiating priest’s nearby home. After a frugal meal and a short rest Vimalananda took me aside to explain some things.

“You are a student of Ayurveda,” he began. “Have you studied Sankhya?” The Sankhya philosophy is the theoretical basis of Ayurveda, and I considered myself to be well-acquainted with its principles, so I replied, “Yes.”

“Then you know that the entire world is made up of the Five Great Elements; you do know the Five Elements?” Yes, I did: Earth, Water, Fire, Air, and Space. These Great Elements are not elements in the chemical sense, like hydrogen and helium; they are rather states of matter. The Earth Element predominates in everything that is solid under normal conditions, the Water Element in that which is liquid, and the Air Element in gases. The Element of Space represents the space in which beings exist and actions occur, and the Fire Element is the force which changes solid to liquid to gas and vice versa. Everything in the manifested universe, including the human being, is made up of these Five Elements.

“As long as you are alive, your consciousness is limited by the vessel in which it is kept: The body. And since the body is made of the Five Elements your consciousness is limited by those very Elements. Knowing that your consciousness is limited is all well and good, but what are you going to do about it? You don’t want to remain limited, do you?”

“Not in the least.”

“Theoretical knowledge is necessary, but not sufficient; practical knowledge, experience is also needed. We have a proverb in Hindi: ‘Where even *ravi* (the sun) cannot reach, there will go a *kavi* (poet).’ Which is true; but it does not go far enough. What I say is, ‘Where even a *kavi* cannot reach, there will go an *anubhavi* (experiencer).’ I believe that you should go out and experience what you have learned, so that it will have some practical value in your life. This is why I have brought you with me today.

“The essence of Tantra is purification of the Five Elements, to awaken the Kundalini Shakti, which is your own personal Shakti (power, energy). Any spiritual practice, in any religion, is basically some process or other of awakening Kundalini, and Kundalini can only be awakened once the Elements in your body have become purified. Do you understand?”

I kept quiet in a way that effectively replied, “Somewhat.”

“I will explain all of this to you—eventually. For now, just think about the Five Elements. You can make spiritual progress by worshiping any of these Elements, but I think it is best to worship the Fire Element. Worshiping Earth may take you eons, because the chief characteristic of Earth is its stability. Worship of Water is unwise nowadays because Water is the main substance which makes up the body, and most of us identify too strongly with our bodies anyway. Worshiping Air directly is likely to make you seriously unstable, and there are difficulties in worshiping Space as well. I think Fire is best.

“The first word in the Rg Veda, the most ancient of the Vedas, is *agni*, fire. The Vedic religion is basically a religion of fire worship. The *rishis*, the Seers who wrote the Vedas, worship the fire because it is the representative of the sun on Earth. Life could not exist on Earth were it not for the sun, and most people think the rishis are trying to propitiate the sun by their fire worship. In fact, they are feeding the sun. If they were ever to stop their continuous offerings of nourishment to the sun, all creation would go to hell. As a byproduct of this service that they perform for the benefit of all embodied beings, they obtain the might of the sun.”
